practical system for turning Grok Bot into a 24/7 AI workforce\nMost people are going to use Grok Bot wrong.\nThey will create a Bot, connect a bunch of tools, give it something vague like:\n\"Handle my business.\"\nThen they will wait for magic.\nThe first run might look great.\nThe second one too.\nThen the Bot misunderstands one task, edits the wrong thing, trusts a bad source, or gets the right result through a path you would never approve.\nNow the AI employee you wanted to trust needs supervision.\nThat is the real problem with AI agents.\nThe question is no longer only:\nIs the model smart enough?\nA better question is:\nHow much autonomy can you give an agent before the cost of a mistake becomes larger than the time it saves?\nThat is what makes Grok Bot interesting.\nA normal chatbot gives you an answer.\nA Bot can work inside a persistent environment, use tools, move through multiple steps, keep context, and return with completed work instead of instructions for you to finish yourself.\n \nGiving an AI a computer is not enough.\nYou still need to decide what it owns, what it can touch, how success is checked, and where it has to stop.\nI publish practical breakdowns of AI agents, automation and AI systems on Substack: substack.com/@lunarresearcher\n \n1. Start With a Boring Job\nThe first instinct is to give Grok Bot something important.\nDon't.\nYour first task should be small enough that a mistake barely matters.\nBad first jobs:\nSend messages to customers\nManage company finances\nPublish content automatically\nHandle contracts\nRun your entire inbox\nChange production systems\nThe upside is obvious.\nThe failure cost is obvious too.\nInstead, pick something that happens often, takes little time to check, produces a visible result, and can be reversed easily.\n \nOne of the source guides makes the same point: the best starter task happens often enough to test the Bot properly and is cheap to verify when something goes wrong.\nFor example:\nEvery morning, collect the five most important AI-agent announcements from the previous 24 hours, remove duplicates, include sources, and save a short brief.\nYou can check that in a minute.\nWhat you are testing is not raw capability.\n \nYou are testing reliability.\n \nThe bad version looks like this:\n \n \n2. Give the Bot a Finish Line\nCompare these two instructions.\nWeak\nResearch my competitors.\nBetter\nEvery Friday, create a report covering the five most important product, pricing and positioning changes among these competitors during the previous seven days. Include a source and date for every claim, flag anything that cannot be independently verified, and save the result in the competitor-research folder.\nThe first describes an activity.\nThe second tells the Bot what finished work looks like.\nOne useful framework from the source material is a Mission Contract containing the result, inputs, output, schedule, definition of done, constraints and approval gates.\n \n \nA simple setup prompt:\n \nAvoid quality standards like:\nuseful\nprofessional\nhigh quality\ncomprehensive\nimportant\nThey sound specific.\nThey are not.\nInstead of:\nFind good sources.\nUse:\nFind 10 non-duplicate sources published within the previous 90 days. Include publication date, author, URL and the claim each source supports.\nNow the Bot has something it can actually check.\n \n3. Stop Writing Mega-Prompts\nA common reaction to agent mistakes is adding more instructions.\nThe Bot forgets something.\nAdd a paragraph.\nIt chooses the wrong tool.\nAdd a rule.\nIt sends something too early.\nAdd another warning.\nEventually one prompt is trying to be the job description, memory system, workflow, security policy, QA checklist and retry logic at the same time.\nThat gets messy fast.\nKeep the permanent role separate from the current task.\n \nFor example:\n \n \nThat role can stay mostly stable.\nTomorrow's assignment can be a separate message.\nIf something breaks, you can tell whether the problem came from the role, the task, the workflow or the permissions.\n \n4. Build One Chief Before Building Ten Agents\nMulti-agent systems look impressive.\nSo people immediately create:\nChief.\nResearcher.\nStrategist.\nWriter.\nDeveloper.\nDesigner.\nReviewer.\nOperator.\nMarketer.\nAnalyst.\nNow they have ten places where context can disappear.\nEvery extra agent creates another handoff and another place where something can get misunderstood.\nThe source material suggests starting with a coordinator and adding specialists only when the work actually requires different context, tools, expertise or independent verification.\nStart smaller.\n \n \nChief owns intake, decomposition, routing, priorities and escalation.\nResearch owns sources, evidence, contradictions and uncertainty.\nExecution owns the actual deliverable.\nReview checks the result against explicit criteria and sends failed work back to the right agent.\nThe Chief should coordinate.\nIt should not quietly do every job itself.\n \n5. Give the Bot One Key, Not the Whole Keyring\nMore integrations make an agent more capable.\nThey also give it more ways to make expensive mistakes.\nA Bot researching competitors probably does not need access to billing, customer messaging, production databases, every Slack channel and every file you own.\nConnect what the current mission requires.\nAdd more only when a real task proves that access is necessary. The same principle appears throughout the source material.\nI would split permissions into three levels.\n \n \nThe point is not to make the Bot ask for permission every minute.\nIt should finish the safe part first.\nA good run might end like this:\n \nThat is useful autonomy.\n \n6. Shared Computer Does Not Mean Separate Security\nPersistent environments are useful because Bots can share files, browser sessions and working state.\nResearch can prepare something.\nExecution can pick it up.\nReviewer can inspect the result.\nBut separate Bot names do not automatically mean separate security boundaries.\nThe source material specifically warns that shared files and authenticated sessions should be treated as shared environment access, not strong isolation between Bots.\n \nAn instruction saying:\nDo not open finance.\ncan guide behavior.\nIt is not a technical access control.\nIf two agents genuinely need different trust levels, the underlying accounts or environments should reflect that.\n \n7. Teach Workflows by Showing Them\nPeople are often bad at describing what they actually do.\nAsk someone how they qualify leads and they might say:\nI check the company, look at the role, then add the good ones.\nWatch them do it and you find a much richer process.\nThey skip companies without a website.\nThey treat founders differently.\nThey ignore rows missing certain fields.\nThey check another source only when something looks suspicious.\nThey know one export button is broken and use a workaround.\nThose details matter.\nThis is why demonstrating a workflow can be more useful than writing the perfect automation spec from memory.\n \nThe source articles describe turning observed workflows into reusable routines that can later run manually, on a schedule, or from a trigger.\nUse something like this:\n \nDo not show only the perfect case.\nShow what happens when a source cannot be verified.\nWhen data is missing.\nWhen two documents disagree.\nWhen authentication expires.\nWhen the reviewer rejects the result.\nThose cases are usually where the real workflow lives.\n \n8. Inspect the Trail, Not Just the Result\nSuppose Grok Bot produces a perfect report.\nGood.\nNow look at how it produced it.\nA correct output can hide a bad process.\n \nMaybe it used a weak source.\nMaybe it misunderstood one instruction and got lucky.\nMaybe it retried a broken path six times.\nMaybe it skipped something that only happened not to matter this time.\nOne of the source guides recommends watching for moments where the Bot guesses, interprets ambiguity or takes an unexpected route. Those moments should become explicit rules or points where the Bot asks for input.\nThe bad loop:\n \nThe better loop:\n \nIf you only repair the final artifact, you are still doing the agent's job for it.\n \n9. Never Give an Agent an Infinite Loop\nThis instruction sounds reasonable:\nKeep working until the task is finished.\nBut finished according to what?\nHow many retries?\nHow much time?\nHow much money?\nA production loop needs boundaries.\n \nThe source material describes agent loops in the same way: verification, failure feedback, bounded retries and escalation should sit around the model instead of being left entirely to the model's judgment.\nExample:\n \nA Bot that knows when to stop is much easier to trust.\n \n10. Add a Reviewer Before Adding More Autonomy\nThe same agent that created something should not always be the only agent deciding whether it is correct.\n \nThe Reviewer should not say:\nThis could be better.\nIt should return something specific.\n \nNow the graph can move backwards.\n \nThe second source uses the same idea: Reviewer should identify the exact failed criterion and send the work back to the specialist capable of fixing it rather than silently rewriting everything.\n \n11. Do Not Automate the First Successful Run\nA Bot succeeds once.\nGreat.\nRun it again.\nThen again.\nOne clean run does not prove much.\nThe case might have been easy.\nThe site might have behaved perfectly.\nNo unusual input may have appeared.\nUse three runs.\n \nThen measure:\n \nA successful run is an event.\nReliability is a pattern.\n \n12. Put Proven Work on a Schedule or Trigger\nOnce the routine is stable, automation becomes useful.\nThere are two obvious ways to start work.\n \nThe schedule or trigger should start a routine that was already tested.\n \nNot this:\n \nScheduled automation also needs limits.\nThe first source makes an important point here: the realistic failure is often not a forbidden action. It is a permitted action repeated hundreds of times because something upstream changed.\n \nThe Bot does not need to go rogue to cause damage.\nSometimes the source page just changes.\n \n13. Let Autonomy Be Earned\nDo not treat autonomy as one switch.\nUse levels.\n \n \nThe source material proposes a similar evidence-based autonomy ladder.\nPromotion should depend on clean runs.\n \nThis should work in both directions.\nIf quality drops, lower the autonomy level.\nIf an integration changes, lower it.\nIf human corrections start piling up, lower it.\nAutonomy should depend on current reliability.\n \n14. The First Agent Team I Would Build\nI would not start with ten agents.\nI would start here:\n \nChief receives one objective, breaks it into work, finds dependencies and routes tasks.\nResearch returns evidence, sources, contradictions, confidence and open questions.\nBuilder turns verified inputs into the actual artifact.\nReviewer checks the artifact against the Mission Contract.\nDistribution stages the final action.\nAnything public stays behind approval.\nAt this point you no longer have several unrelated chat windows.\nYou have a workflow.\n \n15. Pass Work, Not Entire Conversations\nContext can destroy a multi-agent setup surprisingly fast.\nThe lazy approach is copying the whole conversation into every next agent.\nThen the next agent passes everything again.\nSoon everyone sees old instructions, dead ideas, irrelevant corrections and contradictory context.\nUse a compact handoff instead.\n \nThe source material makes the same distinction: artifacts should contain the details, while handoffs should carry the current state and next action.\nThe next agent needs the state of the job.\nIt does not need everyone's conversation history.\n \n16. Audit the System Every Week\nAlways-on automation degrades quietly.\nInterfaces change.\nCredentials expire.\nSources disappear.\nPriorities move.\nA routine can still run while becoming less useful every week.\nGive every automated workflow a weekly receipt.\n \nThen ask:\n \nIf you would not notice that it disappeared, it probably should not exist.\nThe goal is not to collect as many Bots as possible.\nThe goal is to remove useful work.\n \nThe Real Grok Bot Advantage\nPeople will compare Grok to other models.\nWhich one is smarter?\nWhich one writes better?\nWhich one benchmarks higher?\nThose questions matter.\nBut agents add another layer.\nYou now care about whether intelligence can be turned into reliable execution.\n \nThe interesting shift is simple.\nInstead of asking AI to help with the work, you can start giving it ownership of a result.\nBut that only works when you can answer:\n \nOnce those answers are clear, Grok Bot becomes much more than another chat window.\nIt becomes part of the operating system for your work.\n \nIf you read this far\nFollow my Substack for more AI agents, automation and research: substack.com/@lunarresearcher\nFollow me on X: But recently, one has been the most common of them all. Oil prices are above $100/barrel, borrowing costs are at 2002 levels, and inflation is nearly double the Fed's 2% target. So, HOW is the S&P 500 trading ~2% away from a record high?\nWhat if we told you that the S&P 500 can trade near record highs, while simultaneously being in a bear market?\nThat is exactly what we believe is happening. And we believe it represents one of the most significant opportunities in today's market.\nWe are now witnessing a historic market paradox: The \"Bull-Bear\" Market.\nBelow, we explain exactly what we mean.\nThe Iran War - Day #229\n \nToday marks the 229th day of the Iran War. Since the start of the Iran War, the S&P 500 is up +900 points. And, since the March 30th bottom, the S&P 500 is up nearly +1,500 points, adding +$14 trillion of market cap.\nThe world is also a vastly different place than it was 229 days ago. Inflation has nearly doubled, the Fed is hiking interest rates, and we are in the midst of the biggest energy crisis in world history. And, we are also in the midst of the biggest technological revolution in world history. \nYet, if you ask most investors, the current sentiment is that the current fundamental backdrop has deteriorated compared to where it was 8 months ago. \nThe Macroeconomy - A Historic Shift In Expectations \nAt the start of 2026, the Fed was expected to CUT rates at least 3 times, for a total of 75 basis points this year. \n \nThis was a major component of the bullish thesis for equities, with investors expecting cheaper capital, more investment, and record CapEx by technology companies.\n \nToday, the Fed has already hiked interest rates once this year and markets see another 3 interest rate hikes by June 2027, for a total of +100 basis points including September's rate hike. That's a +175 bps swing in rate expectations. Yet, the S&P 500 is still trading at record highs.  \n \nDriving this shift has been the historic surge in oil prices, with diesel now up over +80% from its low seen in January 2026 and Brent crude oil trading at $106/barrel. \nBorrowing costs have surged as inflation doubled, the Fed is hiking rates, and the US is running a near-$2 trillion deficit. But still, the S&P 500 is at record highs. \nSo, what is happening? \nMost Of The S&P 500 Is Struggling: \nThe reality is that the vast majority of the S&P 500's constituents are not trading anywhere near record high territory. Take a look at our analysis below.  \n \nAs indicated in the chart above, 84% of S&P 500 stocks are now trading at least -10% below their all-time highs. \nReal Estate leads the drawdown, with 97% of stocks down at least -10%, followed by Materials at 96%, Utilities at 94%, and Consumer Discretionary at 92%. \nFurthermore, 59% of S&P 500 stocks are down at least -20% from their highs, 41% are down more than -30%, and 17% are down more than -50%.\nNow, take a look at return contribution since the March 30th bottom: \n \nJust 10 stocks have driven 70% of the S&P 500’s gain since March 30th.  These stocks have added +16 percentage points to the index’s +23% gain over this period. Nvidia, $NVDA, has contributed the most, accounting for 13% of the S&P 500's gain, or +3 percentage points.\nThis is followed by Micron, $MU, Apple, $AAPL, and Microsoft, $MSFT, each accounting for 9% of the S&P 500’s advance, adding +2 percentage points. Return contributions by AMD, $AMD, and Alphabet, $GOOGL, have been 8% and 7%, respectively.\nMeanwhile, the remaining 490 stocks have accounted for just 30% of the S&P 500's gain since March 30th, adding +7 percentage points. \nThe reality is that the MAJORITY of S&P 500 components are, in fact, in a bear market. In our view, for the S&P 500, this is in fact bullish, or at worst, does not matter for now. \nWe explain why, next. \nThe S&P 500 Should Be Above 9,000: \nWithout the Iran War, the S&P 500 could be at 9,000+. \nThis is arguably the most resilient market in history. If the S&P 500 is 2% away from a record high despite inflation nearing 4%, gas prices on the rise, and the Fed HIKING rates, just imagine where the S&P 500 would be if the Iran War ended, oil prices came back down, and rate cuts returned. Or, if it never even happened.\nEarnings growth just over the last 7 months of the Iran War has been historic, with the S&P 500 posting +52% blended earnings growth in Q2 2026.  \n \nThis marks the highest growth rate since Q2 2021, a period where the global economy was emerging from a historic lockdown and the US government handed out $4 trillion in stimulus. \nIn other words, in the midst of a global energy crisis and a rate hike campaign, we are now seeing growth on par with $4+ trillion in stimulus and a global economy that restarted from an effective halt. \nThe reality is that the AI narrative is even stronger than most people realize: the earnings growth is there to support it, even as inflation has been above 2% for 67 consecutive months.  \n \nTake a look at Nvidia's P/E ratio over the last 12 months. Even as the stock is up +21% over this period, the company's P/E ratio is down -46%. The stock now has a Forward P/E ratio of ~24x, nearly in line with the S&P 500's 22x, even as $NVDA has risen +900% in 5 years. \nThis is the story for MANY large-cap technology stocks, the very names that are carrying this market. \nAs shown below, the S&P 500 and the S&P 500 Information Technology sector are now seeing nearly identical Forward P/E ratios. Historically, these stocks have almost always traded at a premium to the broader S&P 500.  \n \nIn our view, the very names that have been driving the equity market higher, most large-cap technology stocks, actually have deep discounts priced-in to their stocks, even as they trade at or near record highs. We believe these stocks would be trading MUCH higher if it were not for the macroeconomic factors that we have seen over the last seven months. \nCombine this with the fact that over 80% of S&P 500 stocks are down -10% or more from their record highs, and our thesis is even stronger. If market breadth were to widen even slightly, buying pressure increases rapidly. \nAnd, even without this shift, the large-cap technology stocks that are driving the majority of price action appear relatively \"cheap\" compared to the S&P 500. \nThis is why we said for the S&P 500, market concentration is bullish, or at worst, does not matter *for now.* \nMarch 30th Was A Gift To Investors: \nThe thesis we outline today has been a central part of our investment strategy in our premium research since March 30th. Below is the front page of The Kobeissi Letter as published on March 30th, 2026.  \n \nAs the S&P 500 fell below 6,400, we initiated a strong buy signal on the index and the equity market more broadly. Below is our note on the S&P 500 from March 30th.  \n \nWe noted that the bond market was a central part of our thesis along with the fact that the technology stocks that were driving the broader equity market lower were being sold with seemingly irrational price action. \nThe result was a \"V-Shaped\" recovery in the S&P 500, after which we continuously raised our S&P 500 target to 7,800, and we also called for bond market intervention by the Trump Administration. \nEvery Sunday, we update the above analysis in our weekly research and we also provide daily updates on our private X feed. If you are not already a client, consider subscribing to receive analysis like this in real-time here.\nToday, the Trump Administration is intervening in the bond market, so far unsuccessfully, but further attempts at intervention are inevitable in our view. \nUltimately, however, our thesis today is rooted in our view expressed on March 30th, which has only gained more momentum as we look ahead to 2027. \nInvestors Want More Assets: \nOn top of the discount that is already priced-in to equity markets as discussed above, investors are keen to own assets amid the persistent inflation we are seeing in the US. \nUS CPI inflation has now been above the Fed's 2% target for 67 consecutive months. The last time inflation was below 2% in the US was in January 2021, amid a global economic lockdown. \n \nOver this period, the value of the US Dollar has declined by -22.5%. In other words, it now costs $129 to purchase the same goods and services that cost $100 in 2021. Hence, investors want more stocks and hard assets over the long-run, not less. \nTherefore, if there were to be any indication of the Iran War ending and the global energy crisis subsiding, this would lead to a domino effect in the bullish direction. \nEnergy prices would drop, inflation expectations would follow, the Fed could pivot more dovish, the \"discount\" in technology stocks would be priced-out, and the market's rally would broaden. \nAll while the AI Revolution continues to accelerate and many large-cap technology stocks become cheaper on a forward earnings basis, even as their stock prices rise. \nAnd in the background, the demand for asset ownership is historically robust.\nIn Conclusion: The \"Bull-Bear Market.\" \nIn summary, we believe the S&P 500 is in a bear market, within a bull market.  \n \nEven as the S&P 500 hits year #5 of its bull market, we believe a steep discount is currently priced into equities. This discount has functioned as a \"stealth\" bear market, suppressing equity valuations in the world's largest and fastest-growing companies. \nThis backdrop has the ability to catalyze a violent rally in the S&P 500 if and when it is ultimately priced-out. \nWe expect a highly eventful 2027. \nFollow us @KobeissiLetter for real time analysis as this develops. \nMore about our strategy: Those who are able to remain objective and follow a process during current times of disruption are realizing some of the best trading conditions ever. \nAn objective and systematic approach is what has led to our outperformance of market benchmarks. \nAs shown below, our investment strategy has returned nearly five times the S&P 500 since 2020.  \n \nIf you are interested in receiving our premium daily analysis, you may do so by joining our service here.","preview_text":"We receive thousands of questions per week.
